### Question 58: Congenital Nephrogenic Diabetes Insipidus: AVPR2 (X-Linked) vs AQP2 (Autosomal) Mutations
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Differentiating the genetic basis (AVPR2 vs AQP2 mutations) and inheritance patterns (X-linked vs autosomal recessive/dominant) of congenital nephrogenic diabetes insipidus (NDI), understanding the molecular mechanisms of AQP2 trafficking and function, and relating genotype to phenotype.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 2-month-old male infant presents to the emergency department with severe dehydration, irritability, and failure to thrive. His mother reports excessive thirst (polydipsia) and large volumes of dilute urine (polyuria) since birth. Vital signs show tachycardia (160 bpm), hypotension (70/40 mmHg), and hyperthermia (38.5°C). Physical examination reveals sunken fontanelles and poor skin turgor. Laboratory studies show serum sodium of 155 mEq/L (normal 135-145), serum osmolality of 320 mOsm/kg (normal 275-295), plasma creatinine of 0.8 mg/dL (normal 0.2-0.5), BUN of 30 mg/dL (normal 5-20), and urine specific gravity of 1.005 (normal 1.005-1.030). A water deprivation test was performed, showing failure of urine osmolality to concentrate beyond 200 mOsm/kg despite rising plasma osmolality to 310 mOsm/kg. Administration of desmopressin (DDAVP) resulted in no significant increase in urine osmolality (remained <250 mOsm/kg). Family history reveals that the patient's maternal uncle had a similar condition diagnosed in infancy. Genetic testing reveals an inactivating missense mutation in the AVPR2 gene located on chromosome Xq28 in this patient.

A separate, unrelated 3-month-old female infant presents with identical symptoms of severe polyuria and hypernatremia since birth. Her parents are first cousins. Genetic testing reveals a homozygous frameshift mutation in a gene located on chromosome 12q13.

Which of the following genes, located on chromosome 12q13, is mutated in both autosomal recessive and autosomal dominant forms of congenital nephrogenic diabetes insipidus?

(A) AVPR2
(B) AQP2
(C) AQP3
(D) TRPC6
(E) GNAS1

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient 1 (Male):** Presents with classic symptoms of congenital NDI (polyuria, polydipsia, hypernatremia, dehydration) from birth. Family history (maternal uncle) suggests X-linked inheritance. Genetic testing confirms AVPR2 mutation on Xq28, consistent with X-linked NDI. This case establishes the phenotype of NDI and the genetic basis of the X-linked form.
- **Patient 2 (Female):** Presents with identical symptoms of congenital NDI. Consanguineous parents strongly suggest autosomal recessive inheritance. Genetic testing identifies a mutation on chromosome 12q13.
- **Question:** Asks which gene on chromosome 12q13 is responsible for *both* autosomal recessive and autosomal dominant forms of congenital NDI.
- **Key Clue:** The location of the gene mutation in Patient 2 (chromosome 12q13) is linked to the question about the gene responsible for both autosomal recessive and dominant NDI.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (神经垂体机制与电解质水动力学推导)
- **Nephrogenic Diabetes Insipidus (NDI):** Characterized by the kidney's inability to concentrate urine in response to ADH (vasopressin). This is due to defects in the renal collecting duct's response to ADH.
- **ADH Action:** ADH binds to V2 receptors on the basolateral membrane of principal cells in the collecting duct. This activates Gs protein, leading to increased adenylyl cyclase activity, elevated intracellular cAMP, and activation of Protein Kinase A (PKA). PKA phosphorylates serine residues on AQP2 channels, triggering their translocation from intracellular vesicles to the apical membrane, where they facilitate water reabsorption.
- **Genetic Basis of NDI:**
    - **X-linked NDI (~90%):** Caused by mutations in the *AVPR2* gene (encoding the V2 receptor) located on chromosome Xq28. Mutations are typically inactivating, preventing receptor function and downstream signaling. This leads to a complete lack of response to ADH.
    - **Autosomal Recessive NDI (~9%):** Caused by mutations in the *AQP2* gene (encoding the Aquaporin-2 water channel) located on chromosome 12q13. These mutations are typically inactivating, leading to misfolding and degradation of the AQP2 protein (ERAD), preventing its trafficking to the apical membrane. This results in a severe defect in water reabsorption.
    - **Autosomal Dominant NDI (~1%):** Also caused by mutations in the *AQP2* gene (chromosome 12q13), but these mutations specifically affect the C-terminal cytoplasmic tail of AQP2. These mutant AQP2 monomers can assemble into heterotetramers with wild-type AQP2 monomers. This mistargets the entire complex to the basolateral membrane or lysosomes, preventing apical insertion and function. This is a dominant-negative effect.
- **Other Genes:**
    - *AQP3* and *AQP4*: Located on the basolateral membrane, involved in water transport but not directly regulated by ADH in the same way as AQP2. Mutations are not typically associated with congenital NDI.
    - *TRPC6*: Encodes a calcium channel involved in magnesium transport in the thick ascending limb. Mutations cause familial hypomagnesemia with neonatal seizures, not NDI.
    - *GNAS1*: Encodes the alpha subunit of the stimulatory G protein (Gsα). Mutations cause Albright hereditary osteodystrophy (pseudohypoparathyroidism type Ia) and McCune-Albright syndrome, not NDI.
- **Conclusion:** The *AQP2* gene on chromosome 12q13 is responsible for both the autosomal recessive (inactivating mutations leading to ERAD) and autosomal dominant (C-terminal mutations leading to mistargeting) forms of congenital NDI.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1. Identify the condition: Both infants present with classic signs and symptoms of congenital nephrogenic diabetes insipidus (severe polyuria, polydipsia, hypernatremia, dehydration, failure to concentrate urine despite ADH stimulation).
2. Recognize the genetic basis: Patient 1 has X-linked NDI due to an AVPR2 mutation (Xq28). Patient 2 has autosomal recessive NDI due to a mutation on chromosome 12q13.
3. Recall the genes associated with NDI: X-linked NDI involves AVPR2. Autosomal NDI involves AQP2.
4. Understand the inheritance patterns of AQP2 mutations: AQP2 mutations cause both autosomal recessive (inactivating mutations) and autosomal dominant (C-terminal mutations) NDI.
5. Connect the chromosome location to the gene: The question asks for the gene on chromosome 12q13 responsible for both autosomal recessive and dominant NDI. This points directly to AQP2.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) AVPR2:** This gene is located on chromosome Xq28 and is responsible for X-linked NDI, as seen in Patient 1. It is not located on chromosome 12q13 and is not associated with autosomal NDI. Incorrect.
- **(B) AQP2:** This gene is located on chromosome 12q13. Mutations in AQP2 cause both autosomal recessive NDI (due to protein misfolding and degradation) and autosomal dominant NDI (due to C-terminal mutations causing mistargeting). This matches the description in the question regarding the gene on chromosome 12q13 responsible for both forms. Correct.
- **(C) AQP3:** This gene encodes an aquaporin found primarily on the basolateral membrane of collecting duct cells. While important for water transport, mutations are not a common cause of congenital NDI. Incorrect.
- **(D) TRPC6:** This gene encodes a calcium channel involved in magnesium reabsorption in the thick ascending limb. Mutations cause familial hypomagnesemia, not NDI. Incorrect.
- **(E) GNAS1:** This gene encodes the alpha subunit of the Gs protein. Mutations cause pseudohypoparathyroidism type Ia and McCune-Albright syndrome, not NDI. Incorrect.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)
| Gene     | Chromosome Location | Inheritance Pattern(s) | Mechanism                                                                 |
| :------- | :------------------ | :--------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| AVPR2    | Xq28                | X-linked Recessive     | V2 receptor dysfunction (no ADH signaling)                               |
| AQP2     | 12q13               | Autosomal Recessive, Autosomal Dominant | Protein misfolding/degradation (Recessive) or Mistargeting (Dominant) |
| AQP3/AQP4 | Various             | Rare/Uncommon          | Basolateral water channels, not primary ADH target                        |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
Congenital nephrogenic diabetes insipidus (NDI) can be caused by mutations in different genes, leading to distinct inheritance patterns. X-linked NDI is caused by mutations in the AVPR2 gene (V2 receptor), while both autosomal recessive and autosomal dominant NDI are caused by mutations in the AQP2 gene (aquaporin-2 water channel). Understanding these genetic distinctions is crucial for diagnosis, genetic counseling, and predicting disease severity.

---

### Question 59: Lithium-Induced Nephrogenic DI: ENaC Entry and GSK-3beta Inhibition
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Lithium-induced nephrogenic diabetes insipidus (NDI) pathophysiology, specifically the mechanism of lithium entry into collecting duct principal cells via the epithelial sodium channel (ENaC) and its subsequent downstream effects on aquaporin-2 (AQP2) regulation and function.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 48-year-old man with a 12-year history of bipolar I disorder, well-controlled on lithium carbonate 900 mg daily (serum lithium level 0.9 mEq/L, within therapeutic range), presents with worsening polyuria (average 6.5 L/day) and compensatory polydipsia over the past 6 months. He denies recent changes in medication, fluid intake, or diet. His past medical history is significant only for bipolar disorder. Vital signs are stable (BP 130/70 mmHg, HR 72 bpm). Physical examination reveals no signs of dehydration or volume overload. Laboratory investigations show serum sodium 145 mEq/L (normal), serum osmolality 290 mOsm/kg (normal), plasma vasopressin level 2.5 pg/mL (normal), serum creatinine 1.1 mg/dL (normal), and serum potassium 4.2 mEq/L (normal). Urine analysis reveals a specific gravity of 1.005 and osmolality of 160 mOsm/kg despite serum osmolality being within the normal range. A water deprivation test was performed, showing no significant increase in urine osmolality (>10 mOsm/kg increase). Subsequently, 4 mcg of desmopressin was administered intravenously, resulting in only a minimal increase in urine osmolality to 165 mOsm/kg. These findings are consistent with nephrogenic diabetes insipidus (NDI). The question asks for the specific apical ion channel through which lithium enters the collecting duct principal cells to exert its nephrotoxic effect.

(A) Transient Receptor Potential Vanilloid 5 (TRPV5)
(B) Aquaporin-2 (AQP2)
(C) Epithelial Sodium Channel (ENaC)
(D) Cystic Fibrosis Transmembrane Conductance Regulator (CFTR)
(E) Potassium channel (ROMK)

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 48-year-old male with bipolar disorder on long-term lithium therapy. This immediately raises suspicion for lithium-induced side effects, particularly NDI.
- **Symptoms:** Polyuria (6.5 L/day) and polydipsia are classic symptoms of diabetes insipidus (DI).
- **Laboratory Findings:**
    - Serum lithium 0.9 mEq/L: Therapeutic level, but chronic exposure can still cause toxicity.
    - Serum Na 145 mEq/L: Normal, suggesting euvolemia or mild hypernatremia (compensated by thirst).
    - Urine osmolality 160 mOsm/kg: Inappropriately low for serum osmolality (290 mOsm/kg), indicating impaired concentrating ability.
    - Water deprivation test: Failure to concentrate urine confirms DI.
    - Desmopressin challenge: Minimal response (160 to 165 mOsm/kg) confirms nephrogenic (kidney resistance) rather than central (pituitary deficiency) DI.
- **Diagnosis:** Lithium-induced Nephrogenic Diabetes Insipidus (NDI).
- **Question:** Mechanism of lithium entry into collecting duct principal cells.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (神经垂体机制与电解质水动力学推导)
- **Lithium and NDI:** Lithium is a known cause of NDI. It interferes with the action of ADH (vasopressin) on the collecting duct.
- **Target Cell:** The primary site of action for ADH-mediated water reabsorption is the principal cell of the collecting duct.
- **ADH Signaling:** ADH binds to V2 receptors on the basolateral membrane of principal cells. This activates Gs protein, leading to increased adenylyl cyclase activity, increased intracellular cAMP, and activation of Protein Kinase A (PKA). PKA phosphorylates AQP2 water channels, causing their translocation from intracellular vesicles to the apical membrane, increasing water permeability and reabsorption.
- **Lithium's Mechanism:**
    - **Entry:** Lithium ($Li^+$) is a monovalent cation similar in size and charge to sodium ($Na^+$). It enters the principal cell across the apical membrane primarily through the **Epithelial Sodium Channel (ENaC)**. ENaC is constitutively open and permeable to Na+ and, to a lesser extent, K+ and Li+.
    - **Intracellular Accumulation:** Once inside the cell, lithium is not efficiently pumped out by the basolateral Na+/K+-ATPase (which primarily transports Na+ out and K+ in). This leads to a gradual accumulation of lithium within the principal cell over time with chronic exposure.
    - **Molecular Target:** Intracellular lithium inhibits **Glycogen Synthase Kinase-3 Beta (GSK-3beta)**. GSK-3beta is a crucial kinase involved in various cellular processes, including the regulation of AQP2 gene transcription and the V2 receptor signaling pathway.
    - **Downstream Effects:** Inhibition of GSK-3beta leads to:
        - Decreased transcription of the AQP2 gene.
        - Impaired trafficking and insertion of AQP2 channels into the apical membrane.
        - Reduced number of AQP2 channels at the apical membrane.
        - Overall decrease in the cell's ability to respond to ADH, resulting in impaired water reabsorption and NDI.
- **Other Channels/Proteins:**
    - **TRPV5:** A calcium channel primarily involved in Ca2+ reabsorption in the distal convoluted tubule and collecting duct. Not the primary entry point for lithium.
    - **AQP2:** The water channel itself. Lithium interferes with its regulation and trafficking, but it's not the entry channel for lithium.
    - **CFTR:** A chloride channel involved in Cl- and HCO3- reabsorption in the collecting duct. Not involved in lithium entry.
    - **ROMK:** A potassium channel involved in K+ secretion in the collecting duct. Not involved in lithium entry.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1. The patient presents with symptoms (polyuria, polydipsia) and lab findings (low urine osmolality despite normal serum osmolality, failure to concentrate urine on water deprivation, lack of response to desmopressin) consistent with Nephrogenic Diabetes Insipidus (NDI).
2. The patient has a long history of lithium use, a well-known cause of NDI.
3. The question asks for the mechanism by which lithium enters the target cells (collecting duct principal cells) to cause NDI.
4. Based on the known pathophysiology of lithium-induced NDI, lithium enters the principal cells primarily via the ENaC channel located on the apical membrane.
5. Evaluate the options:
    - (A) TRPV5 is a calcium channel. Incorrect.
    - (B) AQP2 is the water channel regulated by lithium. Incorrect.
    - (C) ENaC is the epithelial sodium channel, known to be the primary route for lithium entry. Correct.
    - (D) CFTR is a chloride channel. Incorrect.
    - (E) ROMK is a potassium channel. Incorrect.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) Transient Receptor Potential Vanilloid 5 (TRPV5):** TRPV5 is a calcium-selective channel primarily expressed in the distal convoluted tubule and connecting tubule, playing a role in calcium reabsorption. While calcium signaling can influence AQP2 trafficking, TRPV5 is not the primary route for lithium entry into collecting duct principal cells. Lithium's entry is primarily driven by its similarity to sodium.
- **(B) Aquaporin-2 (AQP2):** AQP2 is the water channel whose function and expression are inhibited by lithium. It facilitates water movement across the apical membrane, but it is not an ion channel and does not mediate lithium entry. Lithium's effect is downstream of AQP2 regulation.
- **(C) Epithelial Sodium Channel (ENaC):** ENaC is a heterotrimeric channel (alpha, beta, gamma subunits) located on the apical membrane of principal cells in the distal nephron and collecting duct. It is constitutively open and permeable to Na+ and, to a lesser extent, K+ and Li+. Due to the similarity between Li+ and Na+, lithium readily enters the cell through ENaC. This is the established mechanism for lithium's entry into the target cells.
- **(D) Cystic Fibrosis Transmembrane Conductance Regulator (CFTR):** CFTR is a chloride and bicarbonate channel primarily involved in fluid secretion and electrolyte transport. Mutations in CFTR cause cystic fibrosis. It is not involved in lithium transport or the pathophysiology of lithium-induced NDI.
- **(E) Potassium channel (ROMK):** ROMK (Renal Outer Medullary Potassium channel) is a potassium channel involved in potassium secretion in the collecting duct. It is not involved in lithium entry.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)
| Feature             | Lithium-Induced NDI                                  |
|----------------------|------------------------------------------------------|
| **Cause**           | Chronic lithium exposure                               |
| **Target Cell**      | Collecting duct principal cell                         |
| **Entry Mechanism** | Via Epithelial Sodium Channel (ENaC)                   |
| **Intracellular Target**| Glycogen Synthase Kinase-3 Beta (GSK-3beta)          |
| **Downstream Effect**| Decreased AQP2 transcription & trafficking           |
| **Result**          | Impaired water reabsorption, polyuria, NDI             |
| **Diagnostic Test** | Water deprivation + Desmopressin challenge (no response)|
| **Treatment**       | Amiloride (blocks ENaC), fluid restriction             |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
Lithium-induced nephrogenic diabetes insipidus results from the entry of lithium into collecting duct principal cells via the epithelial sodium channel (ENaC), followed by inhibition of GSK-3beta, leading to decreased AQP2 expression and function, ultimately impairing the kidney's ability to concentrate urine in response to ADH. Understanding this specific entry mechanism via ENaC is crucial for comprehending the pathophysiology and targeted treatment (amiloride) of this condition.

---

### Question 60: Amiloride Therapy for Lithium-Induced NDI: Selective Apical ENaC Blockade
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Mechanism of action of amiloride in treating lithium-induced nephrogenic diabetes insipidus (NDI), specifically its effect on epithelial sodium channels (ENaC) and intracellular lithium accumulation. (阿米洛राइड治疗锂致性难治性尿崩症的作用机制，特别是其对上皮钠通道(ENaC)和细胞内锂积累的影响)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old woman with bipolar disorder, effectively managed on lithium therapy for 10 years, presents with worsening polyuria and polydipsia over the past 6 months. Her daily urine output has increased to 7.0 L, and she experiences significant nocturia. She denies other medications or changes in fluid intake. Her past medical history is notable only for bipolar disorder. Vital signs are stable: BP 125/75 mmHg, HR 72 bpm, RR 16/min, Temp 37°C. Physical examination reveals signs of mild dehydration (dry mucous membranes, decreased skin turgor). Laboratory results show: Serum Sodium 148 mEq/L (normal 135-145), Serum Potassium 4.0 mEq/L (normal 3.5-5.0), Serum Creatinine 1.1 mg/dL (normal 0.6-1.2), Plasma Osmolality 305 mOsm/kg (normal 275-295), Urine Osmolality 120 mOsm/kg (normal 500-1200, inappropriately low given plasma osmolality), Urine Specific Gravity 1.003 (normal 1.005-1.030), Urine Sodium 25 mEq/L. A water deprivation test confirms the inability to concentrate urine beyond 150 mOsm/kg despite rising plasma osmolality to 315 mOsm/kg. Given her dependence on lithium for mood stabilization, her psychiatrist and nephrologist decide to add amiloride 5 mg daily. After 2 weeks, her 24-hour urine volume decreases to 3.2 L, and urine osmolality increases to 340 mOsm/kg. What is the specific molecular target and mechanism of action of amiloride in treating lithium-induced nephrogenic diabetes insipidus?

(A) Inhibition of aquaporin-2 (AQP2) water channel insertion into the apical membrane of collecting duct principal cells, thereby reducing water reabsorption.
(B) Blockade of the apical epithelial sodium channel (ENaC), preventing lithium entry into principal cells and restoring normal vasopressin signaling.
(C) Stimulation of the basolateral Na+/K+-ATPase pump, increasing intracellular sodium concentration and driving water reabsorption via AQP2.
(D) Inhibition of the urea transporter UT-A1 in the inner medullary collecting duct, reducing urea recycling and impairing the medullary osmotic gradient.
(E) Activation of the V2 vasopressin receptor (V2R) on collecting duct principal cells, increasing intracellular cAMP and promoting AQP2 translocation.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old woman with bipolar disorder on long-term lithium therapy.
- **Presenting Complaint:** Polyuria (7.0 L/day), polydipsia, nocturia, suggestive of diabetes insipidus.
- **Physical Exam:** Mild dehydration signs (dry mucous membranes, decreased skin turgor), consistent with excessive water loss.
- **Laboratory Findings:**
    - Hypernatremia (148 mEq/L) and elevated plasma osmolality (305 mOsm/kg) indicate relative water deficit.
    - Inappropriately low urine osmolality (120 mOsm/kg) despite hyperosmolality, indicating impaired urine concentrating ability.
    - Low urine specific gravity (1.003) confirms dilute urine.
    - Urine sodium (25 mEq/L) is not particularly high or low, suggesting euvolemia or mild dehydration rather than volume depletion or overload.
- **Water Deprivation Test:** Confirms nephrogenic diabetes insipidus (NDI) as urine fails to concentrate adequately despite rising plasma osmolality.
- **Treatment:** Addition of amiloride leads to a significant reduction in urine volume (7.0 L to 3.2 L) and an increase in urine osmolality (120 to 340 mOsm/kg).
- **Question:** Asks for the specific molecular target and mechanism of amiloride in this context.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (神经垂体机制与电解质水动力学推导)
- **Lithium and NDI:** Lithium is a mood stabilizer commonly used for bipolar disorder. A significant side effect is NDI, occurring in 15-30% of patients. Lithium enters principal cells of the collecting duct via the apical epithelial sodium channel (ENaC).
- **Intracellular Lithium Effects:** Once inside the cell, lithium inhibits adenylyl cyclase, reducing intracellular cAMP levels. This interferes with the normal signaling cascade initiated by vasopressin (ADH) binding to the V2 receptor. Specifically, it impairs the phosphorylation of aquaporin-2 (AQP2) water channels, preventing their translocation from intracellular vesicles to the apical membrane. This reduces water reabsorption capacity, leading to polyuria and dilute urine. Lithium also inhibits glycogen synthase kinase-3 beta (GSK-3β), which is involved in AQP2 regulation.
- **Amiloride Mechanism:** Amiloride is a potassium-sparing diuretic. Its primary mechanism is the direct blockade of the ENaC located on the apical membrane of principal cells in the late distal tubule and collecting duct.
- **Amiloride in Lithium-Induced NDI:** By blocking ENaC, amiloride prevents lithium ions ($Li^+$) from entering the principal cells from the tubular lumen. This reduces the intracellular lithium concentration, alleviating its inhibitory effects on adenylyl cyclase and GSK-3β. Consequently, the normal vasopressin signaling pathway is restored, leading to increased intracellular cAMP, AQP2 phosphorylation, and apical membrane insertion, thereby enhancing water reabsorption and concentrating the urine.
- **Receptor Signaling:**
    - V2 Receptor (V2R): Gs-coupled receptor. Binding of ADH activates adenylyl cyclase, increasing intracellular cAMP. cAMP activates protein kinase A (PKA). PKA phosphorylates AQP2 channels, targeting them for insertion into the apical membrane via exocytosis of intracellular vesicles.
    - ENaC: A heterotrimeric ion channel (α, β, γ subunits) responsible for sodium reabsorption in the late distal tubule and collecting duct. Amiloride binds to the extracellular vestibule of the ENaC pore, physically blocking sodium and lithium entry.
- **Other Options:**
    - (A) AQP2 inhibition would worsen NDI.
    - (C) Na+/K+-ATPase is basolateral and not the target of amiloride; its stimulation wouldn't directly address the AQP2 trafficking issue caused by lithium.
    - (D) UT-A1 is involved in urea recycling; inhibiting it would impair the medullary gradient but is not the mechanism of amiloride.
    - (E) V2R activation is the goal, but lithium inhibits this pathway downstream; amiloride works upstream by preventing lithium entry, thus allowing V2R signaling to function normally.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1.  The patient presents with classic symptoms and lab findings of nephrogenic diabetes insipidus (polyuria, polydipsia, hypernatremia, high plasma osmolality, low urine osmolality) in the context of long-term lithium therapy.
2.  Lithium is known to cause NDI by entering collecting duct cells via ENaC and interfering with the ADH signaling pathway, specifically AQP2 trafficking.
3.  Amiloride is added and successfully reduces urine output and increases urine osmolality, indicating it is treating the NDI.
4.  Amiloride is a known ENaC blocker.
5.  Therefore, amiloride must be working by blocking ENaC to prevent lithium entry into the cells, thereby reversing the lithium-induced impairment of water reabsorption.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) Inhibition of aquaporin-2 (AQP2) water channel insertion...**: This describes the *effect* of lithium, not the mechanism of amiloride. Amiloride *restores* AQP2 function, it doesn't inhibit it. Incorrect.
- **(B) Blockade of the apical epithelial sodium channel (ENaC), preventing lithium entry into principal cells and restoring normal vasopressin signaling**: This accurately describes the mechanism of amiloride in lithium-induced NDI. Amiloride blocks ENaC, preventing lithium influx, which allows the normal ADH/V2R/cAMP/AQP2 pathway to function. Correct.
- **(C) Stimulation of the basolateral Na+/K+-ATPase pump...**: The Na+/K+-ATPase is crucial for maintaining the electrochemical gradient for sodium entry via ENaC, but amiloride does not directly stimulate it. Furthermore, stimulating this pump wouldn't address the intracellular lithium accumulation or AQP2 trafficking defect. Incorrect.
- **(D) Inhibition of the urea transporter UT-A1...**: UT-A1 is involved in urea recycling in the inner medullary collecting duct, contributing to the medullary osmotic gradient. Amiloride does not target UT-A1. Incorrect.
- **(E) Activation of the V2 vasopressin receptor (V2R) on collecting duct principal cells...**: While the ultimate goal is to enhance water reabsorption via V2R signaling, amiloride does not directly activate the V2R. Lithium interferes with the downstream signaling *after* V2R activation. Amiloride works upstream by preventing lithium from entering the cell and disrupting this pathway. Incorrect.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)
| Feature                 | Lithium Mechanism in NDI                                  | Amiloride Mechanism in Lithium-Induced NDI                     |
| :---------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Target Cell**         | Principal cells of collecting duct                         | Principal cells of collecting duct                              |
| **Entry Mechanism**     | Enters via apical ENaC                                     | Blocks apical ENaC                                              |
| **Intracellular Effect** | Inhibits adenylyl cyclase, reduces cAMP; inhibits GSK-3β | Prevents lithium entry, allowing normal cAMP and GSK-3β function |
| **Downstream Effect**   | Impairs AQP2 phosphorylation & trafficking                | Restores AQP2 phosphorylation & trafficking                     |
| **Net Result**          | Decreased water reabsorption (NDI)                        | Increased water reabsorption (treatment of NDI)                 |
| **Primary Target**      | Adenylyl cyclase, GSK-3β, AQP2 trafficking               | ENaC                                                           |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
Lithium-induced nephrogenic diabetes insipidus results from intracellular lithium accumulation via ENaC, which inhibits the ADH signaling pathway and AQP2 water channel function. Amiloride treats this condition by selectively blocking ENaC, preventing lithium entry into collecting duct cells and restoring normal water reabsorption capacity. This highlights the importance of understanding the specific cellular targets and mechanisms of drug interactions in managing endocrine and renal disorders.

### Question 61: Hypercalcemia-Induced Nephrogenic DI: Calcium-Sensing Receptor (CaSR) and Medullary Injury
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Hypercalcemia causes nephrogenic diabetes insipidus (NDI) by activating the calcium-sensing receptor (CaSR) on renal tubular cells, leading to impaired medullary concentrating ability and reduced water reabsorption. (高钙血症通过激活钙感受受体(CaSR)导致肾性糖尿病(NDI)，从而损害髓质浓缩能力和水重吸收。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old woman with a known history of metastatic breast cancer to bone presents to the emergency department complaining of increasing lethargy, constipation, excessive thirst (polydipsia), and passing large volumes of dilute urine (polyuria, approximately 5 L/day) for the past week. Her past medical history is significant for stage IV breast adenocarcinoma treated with palliative chemotherapy. Vital signs show a heart rate of 110 bpm, blood pressure 100/60 mmHg, respiratory rate 18/min, and temperature 37.0°C. Physical examination reveals dry mucous membranes and decreased skin turgor, suggesting volume depletion despite the polyuria. Laboratory results show serum sodium 146 mEq/L, serum calcium 14.2 mg/dL (normal range 8.5-10.5 mg/dL), serum creatinine 1.8 mg/dL (normal range 0.6-1.2 mg/dL), BUN 35 mg/dL (normal range 7-20 mg/dL), plasma osmolality 295 mOsm/kg, and urine osmolality 180 mOsm/kg. A water deprivation test was initiated, but urine osmolality remained low (<300 mOsm/kg) despite increasing plasma osmolality to 305 mOsm/kg. Administration of desmopressin (DDAVP) did not significantly increase urine osmolality (peak Uosm 220 mOsm/kg). Intravenous hydration with normal saline and treatment with bisphosphonates were initiated, leading to a gradual decrease in serum calcium to 9.8 mg/dL over 4 days. Concurrently, the patient's polyuria resolved, and subsequent water deprivation testing showed a normal response with urine osmolality increasing to >600 mOsm/kg. Which mechanism best explains the initial impairment in urine concentrating ability in this patient?

(A) Downregulation of aquaporin-2 (AQP2) water channels in the collecting duct due to chronic lithium exposure.
(B) Impaired synthesis of vasopressin (ADH) from hypothalamic magnocellular neurons due to metastatic disease infiltration.
(C) Activation of the calcium-sensing receptor (CaSR) leading to inhibition of adenylyl cyclase and reduced AQP2 insertion in the collecting duct apical membrane.
(D) Increased expression of epithelial sodium channels (ENaC) in the collecting duct principal cells, leading to excessive sodium reabsorption and osmotic diuresis.
(E) Decreased activity of the Na+/K+-ATPase pump in the basolateral membrane of collecting duct cells, impairing the electrochemical gradient for water reabsorption.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old woman with metastatic breast cancer to bone. This suggests potential hypercalcemia due to bone resorption.
- **Symptoms:** Lethargy, constipation, polyuria (5 L/day), polydipsia. These are classic symptoms of hypercalcemia and associated volume depletion/NDI.
- **Vital Signs/Exam:** Tachycardia, hypotension, dry mucous membranes, decreased skin turgor. Indicate volume depletion.
- **Labs:**
    - Serum Na 146 mEq/L: Mild hypernatremia, consistent with water loss exceeding sodium loss (or inadequate water intake).
    - Serum Ca 14.2 mg/dL: Markedly elevated, confirming hypercalcemia.
    - Serum Creatinine 1.8 mg/dL, BUN 35 mg/dL: Elevated, suggesting prerenal azotemia due to volume depletion.
    - Plasma Osm 295 mOsm/kg: Slightly elevated, consistent with dehydration.
    - Urine Osm 180 mOsm/kg: Inappropriately low given the plasma osmolality and volume depletion. This indicates impaired concentrating ability.
- **Water Deprivation Test:** Urine osmolality remained low (<300 mOsm/kg) despite rising plasma osmolality, ruling out central DI or primary polydipsia.
- **Desmopressin Challenge:** Lack of response (Uosm only increased to 220 mOsm/kg) confirms nephrogenic DI (NDI).
- **Treatment Response:** Normalization of serum calcium with hydration and bisphosphonates led to resolution of polyuria and restoration of normal concentrating ability. This strongly implicates hypercalcemia as the cause of the NDI.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (神经垂体机制与电解质水动力学推导)
- **Hypercalcemia and NDI:** High extracellular calcium levels directly affect renal tubular function.
- **Calcium-Sensing Receptor (CaSR):** This is a G-protein-coupled receptor (GPCR) expressed on various cell types, including renal tubular epithelial cells (especially in the thick ascending limb (TAL) and collecting duct). It senses extracellular calcium concentration.
- **Mechanism in TAL:** CaSR activation in the TAL inhibits the Na+-K+-2Cl- cotransporter (NKCC2) and apical ROMK channels. This reduces NaCl reabsorption in the TAL, impairing the generation and maintenance of the medullary osmotic gradient (countercurrent multiplication). A reduced medullary gradient limits the kidney's ability to concentrate urine.
- **Mechanism in Collecting Duct:** CaSR activation in the principal cells of the collecting duct activates Gi/o proteins, inhibiting adenylyl cyclase and decreasing intracellular cAMP levels. Low cAMP reduces the phosphorylation (at Ser256) and apical insertion of aquaporin-2 (AQP2) water channels. This decreases water permeability and reabsorption in the collecting duct. CaSR can also activate Gq proteins, leading to endocytosis and degradation of AQP2.
- **Reversibility:** The effects of hypercalcemia on CaSR-mediated renal function are generally reversible upon normalization of serum calcium levels, as seen in this patient.
- **Distractor Analysis:**
    - (A) Lithium toxicity causes NDI by inhibiting glycogen synthase kinase-3 beta (GSK-3β), leading to impaired AQP2 trafficking and downregulation of AQP2 expression. It does not involve CaSR. The patient's history doesn't mention lithium use.
    - (B) Central DI involves deficient ADH synthesis or release. This would present with dilute urine, but the water deprivation test would show *no* increase in urine osmolality, and desmopressin would *not* cause a significant increase. The patient's history points to hypercalcemia, not a central cause.
    - (D) Increased ENaC activity is characteristic of aldosterone excess or lithium toxicity, leading to increased sodium reabsorption. While this can contribute to volume expansion and potentially affect water balance, it's not the primary mechanism of hypercalcemia-induced NDI, which directly impairs concentrating ability via CaSR and medullary gradient disruption.
    - (E) Impaired Na+/K+-ATPase activity would generally impair sodium reabsorption throughout the nephron, leading to salt wasting and potentially volume depletion, but it doesn't directly explain the specific impairment of urine concentrating ability seen in hypercalcemia-induced NDI. The primary defect is in water reabsorption due to AQP2 dysregulation and impaired medullary gradient.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1.  Identify the core problem: Polyuria and polydipsia with inappropriately dilute urine (low Uosm) in the setting of volume depletion and hypernatremia.
2.  Recognize the pattern: This suggests diabetes insipidus (DI).
3.  Differentiate DI types: The lack of response to desmopressin points towards nephrogenic DI (NDI).
4.  Identify the underlying cause: The patient has metastatic breast cancer to bone and marked hypercalcemia.
5.  Connect hypercalcemia to NDI: Hypercalcemia is a known cause of NDI.
6.  Determine the mechanism: Hypercalcemia activates the CaSR on renal tubular cells.
7.  Elucidate CaSR effects: CaSR activation inhibits NKCC2 in the TAL (blunting medullary gradient) and inhibits AQP2 insertion/promotes endocytosis in the collecting duct (reducing water reabsorption).
8.  Confirm the mechanism: The patient's NDI resolved completely upon correction of hypercalcemia, strongly supporting this mechanism.
9.  Evaluate the options: Option (C) accurately describes the CaSR-mediated mechanism linking hypercalcemia to impaired urine concentration.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) Downregulation of aquaporin-2 (AQP2) water channels in the collecting duct due to chronic lithium exposure.** Incorrect. Lithium causes NDI, but the mechanism involves GSK-3β inhibition and AQP2 trafficking/expression issues, not CaSR activation. The vignette does not mention lithium use.
- **(B) Impaired synthesis of vasopressin (ADH) from hypothalamic magnocellular neurons due to metastatic disease infiltration.** Incorrect. This describes central DI. The patient's lack of response to desmopressin and the reversibility with calcium correction rule out central DI. Metastatic infiltration is unlikely to selectively impair ADH synthesis without other neurological signs.
- **(C) Activation of the calcium-sensing receptor (CaSR) leading to inhibition of adenylyl cyclase and reduced AQP2 insertion in the collecting duct apical membrane.** Correct. This accurately describes the primary mechanism by which hypercalcemia causes NDI, affecting both medullary gradient formation (via TAL) and water reabsorption (via collecting duct).
- **(D) Increased expression of epithelial sodium channels (ENaC) in the collecting duct principal cells, leading to excessive sodium reabsorption and osmotic diuresis.** Incorrect. Increased ENaC activity is associated with aldosterone excess or lithium toxicity, leading to sodium retention and potentially volume expansion, not the primary defect in hypercalcemia-induced NDI, which is impaired water reabsorption and medullary gradient disruption.
- **(E) Decreased activity of the Na+/K+-ATPase pump in the basolateral membrane of collecting duct cells, impairing the electrochemical gradient for water reabsorption.** Incorrect. Impaired Na+/K+-ATPase would affect sodium reabsorption more broadly and is not the specific mechanism for hypercalcemia-induced NDI. The primary issue is AQP2 function and medullary gradient, not the fundamental electrochemical gradient generation.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)
| Feature                 | Central DI                     | Nephrogenic DI (Hypercalcemia) | Primary Polydipsia             | SIADH                           |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------ | :----------------------------- | :----------------------------- | :------------------------------ |
| **Cause**               | ↓ ADH synthesis/release         | ↓ Renal response to ADH        | Excessive water intake         | ↑ ADH effect (inappropriate)    |
| **Plasma Osm**          | High                            | Normal/High                    | Low                            | Low                             |
| **Urine Osm**           | Low                             | Low                            | Low (but can concentrate)      | High                            |
| **Urine Volume**        | High                            | High                           | High                           | Low                             |
| **Plasma Na**           | High                            | Normal/High                    | Low                            | Low                             |
| **Desmopressin Test**   | ↑ Urine Osm (>50%)              | No significant ↑ Urine Osm     | No significant ↑ Urine Osm     | N/A                             |
| **Water Deprivation**   | No ↑ Urine Osm                  | No ↑ Urine Osm                 | ↑ Urine Osm (>600-800 mOsm/kg) | Urine Osm > Plasma Osm (usually) |
| **Key Mechanism**       | ADH deficiency                  | Renal resistance to ADH        | Excessive intake, ↓ thirst     | ADH excess/sensitivity          |
| **Receptor Involved**   | V2R (brain/kidney)              | CaSR (kidney), V2R (kidney)     | N/A                            | V2R (kidney)                    |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
Hypercalcemia causes nephrogenic diabetes insipidus (NDI) by activating the calcium-sensing receptor (CaSR) on renal tubular cells. This activation inhibits medullary countercurrent multiplication (via NKCC2 inhibition in the TAL) and impairs water reabsorption in the collecting duct (via inhibition of AQP2 insertion/promotion of endocytosis). This mechanism is reversible upon correction of hypercalcemia. Understanding the role of CaSR in renal physiology is crucial for linking systemic calcium abnormalities to specific renal dysfunction.
